      "content": "\\section{Proofs of Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new} and~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}}\n\\label{app:prop_proofs}\n\n\\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\\ref{prop:no_new}]\nSuppose $c_n' = c_n + \\delta$ preserves all response probabilities. Then $\\delta \\cdot e_i = 0$ for all items. Since $e_i \\neq 0$ for at least one item by assumption, $\\delta = 0$. Fixing $e_i$ externally eliminates the bilinear ambiguity $c_n \\cdot e_i = (\\lambda c_n)(\\lambda^{-1} e_i)$ that would otherwise render the decomposition non-unique.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}]\nWhen exposure is constant, the linear predictor becomes $(\\theta_n + c_n \\cdot e) - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k$, equivalent to a standard MFRM with reparameterized ability $\\theta^*_n = \\theta_n + c_n \\cdot e$. No configuration of response data can distinguish $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ from $(\\theta_n + \\epsilon, c_n - \\epsilon/e)$ for any $\\epsilon$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\n\\section{Proof of Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}}\n\\label{app:proof}\n\nWe prove that the G-MFRM is locally identifiable under the conditions of Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}. Following \\citet{rothenberg1971}, a parametric model is locally identifiable at the true parameter vector $\\boldsymbol{\\psi}_0$ if and only if the Fisher information matrix $\\mathbf{F}(\\boldsymbol{\\psi}_0)$ is non-singular.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.} The G-MFRM parameter vector is $\\boldsymbol{\\psi} = (\\boldsymbol{\\theta}, \\mathbf{c}, \\mathbf{b}, \\mathbf{d}, \\boldsymbol{\\tau})$, where $\\boldsymbol{\\theta} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{N-1}$ (after the location constraint $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$), $\\mathbf{c} \\in \\mathbb{R}_{\\geq 0}^N$, $\\mathbf{b} \\in \\mathbb{R}^I$, $\\mathbf{d} \\in \\mathbb{R}^J$, and $\\boldsymbol{\\tau} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{K-1}$. The exposure vector $\\mathbf{e} = (e_1, \\ldots, e_I)$ is fixed externally and treated as known. The linear predictor is $\\eta_{nijk} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i$, with partial derivatives:\n%\n\\begin{align*}\n\\frac{\\partial \\eta_{nijk}}{\\partial \\theta_n} &= 1, \\quad\n\\frac{\\partial \\eta_{nijk}}{\\partial c_n} = e_i, \\quad\n\\frac{\\partial \\eta_{nijk}}{\\partial b_i} = -1, \\\\\n\\frac{\\partial \\eta_{nijk}}{\\partial d_j} &= -1, \\quad\n\\frac{\\partial \\eta_{nijk}}{\\partial \\tau_k} = -1.\n\\end{align*}\n\n\\paragraph{Fisher information weights.} For each observation cell $(n,i,j)$, the Fisher information weight under the cumulative logit model is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:fisher_weight}\nw_{nij} = \\sum_{k=1}^{K} \\frac{\\bigl[\\sigma'(\\eta_{nijk}) - \\sigma'(\\eta_{nij,k+1})\\bigr]^2}{P(Y_{nij} = k)},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $\\sigma(x) = (1 + e^{-x})^{-1}$, $\\sigma'(x) = \\sigma(x)(1 - \\sigma(x))$, and we set $\\sigma'(\\eta_{nij,0}) = \\sigma'(\\eta_{nij,K+1}) = 0$ at the boundaries. Since $P(Y_{nij} = k) > 0$ for all categories and $\\sigma'(\\cdot) > 0$, we have $w_{nij} > 0$ for all $(n,i,j)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Step 1: The $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix.} For each model $n$, the $2 \\times 2$ Fisher information submatrix is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:fisher_block}\n\\mathbf{F}_n = \\sum_{i=1}^{I} \\sum_{j=1}^{J} w_{nij}\n\\begin{pmatrix} 1 & e_i \\\\ e_i & e_i^2 \\end{pmatrix}.\n\\end{equation}\n%\nIts determinant is\n%\n\\begin{multline}\n\\det \\mathbf{F}_n = \\Bigl(\\textstyle\\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\\Bigr)\\Bigl(\\textstyle\\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\\, e_i^2\\Bigr) \\\\\n- \\Bigl(\\textstyle\\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\\, e_i\\Bigr)^2 = W_n^2 \\cdot \\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i),\n\\end{multline}\n%\nwhere $W_n = \\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}$ and $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = \\overline{e^2}_n - \\bar{e}_n^2$ is the weighted variance of exposure with weights $w_{nij}$. By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) \\geq 0$, with equality if and only if $e_i$ is constant across all items with $w_{nij} > 0$. Under conditions~(i) and~(iii), the observed items for model $n$ include at least two distinct exposure values with $w_{nij} > 0$ for the corresponding cells. Therefore $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) > 0$, $\\det \\mathbf{F}_n > 0$, and each $\\mathbf{F}_n$ is positive definite.\n\n\\paragraph{Step 2: Item and judge blocks.} Under the connected design (condition~i), each item $i$ is observed by at least one $(n,j)$ pair, so the item difficulty block has positive diagonal entries $[\\mathbf{F}_{bb}]_{ii} = \\sum_{n,j} w_{nij} > 0$ (summing over observed cells). Similarly, each judge $j$ observes at least one $(n,i)$ pair, giving $[\\mathbf{F}_{dd}]_{jj} = \\sum_{n,i} w_{nij} > 0$. Condition~(ii) ($J \\geq 2$) ensures the judge block has sufficient dimension to separate judge effects from the intercept. Both blocks are positive definite.\n\n\\paragraph{Step 3: Schur complement argument.} The full Fisher information matrix $\\mathbf{F}$ contains off-diagonal blocks coupling the parameter groups. Partition as\n%\n\\begin{equation*}\n\\mathbf{F} = \\begin{pmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^\\top & \\mathbf{D} \\end{pmatrix},\n\\end{equation*}\n%\nwhere $\\mathbf{A} = \\mathrm{blockdiag}(\\mathbf{F}_1, \\ldots, \\mathbf{F}_N)$ collects all $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrices and $\\mathbf{D}$ collects the $(\\mathbf{b}, \\mathbf{d}, \\boldsymbol{\\tau})$ blocks. From Step~1, $\\mathbf{A}$ is positive definite. The Schur complement\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:schur}\n\\mathbf{S} = \\mathbf{D} - \\mathbf{B}^\\top \\mathbf{A}^{-1} \\mathbf{B}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nequals the conditional Fisher information for $(\\mathbf{b}, \\mathbf{d}, \\boldsymbol{\\tau})$ given $(\\boldsymbol{\\theta}, \\mathbf{c})$. Conditioning on $(\\boldsymbol{\\theta}, \\mathbf{c})$ with fixed $\\mathbf{e}$ reduces the model to a standard MFRM with known offsets $c_n \\cdot e_i$, whose identifiability is established by \\citet[Chapter~5]{sanmartin2016} under conditions~(i), (ii), and~(iv). Therefore $\\mathbf{S}$ is positive definite.\n\nSince both $\\mathbf{A}$ and $\\mathbf{S}$ are positive definite:\n%\n\\begin{equation*}\n\\det \\mathbf{F} = \\det \\mathbf{A} \\cdot \\det \\mathbf{S} > 0,\n\\end{equation*}\n%\nestablishing non-singularity of $\\mathbf{F}$ and hence local identifiability. \\qed\n\n\\paragraph{Local vs.\\ global identifiability.} Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient} establishes \\emph{local} identifiability: the Fisher information matrix is non-singular at the true parameter value, guaranteeing that no infinitesimally close parameter vector produces the same likelihood. This does not rule out discrete global non-identifiabilities (i.e., isolated points in parameter space with identical likelihood). However, the HalfNormal prior on $c_n$ restricts the parameter space to $c_n \\geq 0$, eliminating the sign ambiguity that would otherwise create a global reflection $(\\theta_n + \\delta,\\, c_n - \\delta/e_i)$. Combined with the location constraint $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$ and the empirical observation that the posterior is unimodal across all chains (no mode-switching detected), the local result provides a practical guarantee of unique parameter recovery.\n\n\\paragraph{Role of the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality.} The key step in Step~1 is the decomposition $\\det \\mathbf{F}_n = W_n^2 \\cdot \\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i)$, where the weighted variance $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = \\mathbb{E}_w[e_i^2] - (\\mathbb{E}_w[e_i])^2 \\geq 0$ by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality applied to the vectors $(\\sqrt{w_{nij}})$ and $(\\sqrt{w_{nij}}\\, e_i)$. Strict positivity follows from the requirement that $e_i$ takes at least two distinct values over observed items (condition~iii): if $e_i = e$ for all $i$ with $w_{nij} > 0$, then $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = 0$ and the $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ block becomes singular, recovering Proposition~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}. The Cauchy--Schwarz bound is tight only in this degenerate case, so any non-trivial exposure variation suffices for identification.\n\n\n\\section{Explicit Form of the OVB Coefficient $\\gamma_n$}\n\\label{app:gamma}\n\nUnder the MFRM logistic link, a first-order Taylor expansion of Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb} yields an explicit approximation for the bias coefficient $\\gamma_n$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:gamma_explicit}\n\\gamma_n \\approx \\frac{\\sum_{i} w_{ni} \\cdot e_i \\cdot p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})}{\\sum_{i} w_{ni} \\cdot p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $p_{ni} = \\sigma(\\theta_n - b_i - \\bar{d})$ is the MFRM predicted probability averaged over judges and $w_{ni}$ is the effective item weight (number of judge observations for item $i$ on model $n$). Eq.~\\ref{eq:gamma_explicit} is a weighted mean of the exposure values $e_i$, with weights proportional to the Fisher information $p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})$. Items near the boundary ($p_{ni} \\approx 0.5$) contribute more to the bias than items at the extremes, because the logistic link is most sensitive at these points. This approximation is accurate to first order in $(c_n \\cdot e_i)$; the synthetic validation in Section~\\ref{sec:confounding} ($r = 0.86$) confirms that the linear approximation captures the dominant bias structure.\n\n\n\\section{Murphy--Topel Variance Correction}\n\\label{app:murphy_topel}\n\nThe three-stage G-MFRM procedure treats Stage~2 exposure estimates $\\hat{e}_i$ as fixed in Stage~3, understating posterior uncertainty by ignoring estimation error in $\\hat{e}_i$. We derive the Murphy--Topel correction \\citep{murphy1985} adapted to the G-MFRM setting.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.} Let $\\boldsymbol{\\alpha} = (e_1, \\ldots, e_I)$ denote the Stage~2 parameters estimated from an independent dataset with log-likelihood $\\ell_2(\\boldsymbol{\\alpha})$, and let $\\boldsymbol{\\psi} = (\\boldsymbol{\\theta}, \\mathbf{c}, \\mathbf{b}, \\mathbf{d}, \\boldsymbol{\\tau})$ denote the Stage~3 parameters estimated with log-likelihood $\\ell_3(\\boldsymbol{\\psi}; \\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}})$, where $\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}}$ is plugged in as known.\n\n\\paragraph{Naive and Stage~2 variances.} The naive (uncorrected) variance of $\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\psi}}$ is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathbf{V}_3 = \\left[-\\frac{\\partial^2 \\ell_3}{\\partial \\boldsymbol{\\psi}\\, \\partial \\boldsymbol{\\psi}^\\top}\\bigg|_{\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\psi}},\\, \\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}}}\\right]^{-1},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nand the Stage~2 variance is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathbf{V}_2 = \\left[-\\frac{\\partial^2 \\ell_2}{\\partial \\boldsymbol{\\alpha}\\, \\partial \\boldsymbol{\\alpha}^\\top}\\bigg|_{\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}}}\\right]^{-1}.\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-derivative matrix.} The coupling between stages is captured by the cross-derivative\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathbf{C} = \\frac{\\partial^2 \\ell_3}{\\partial \\boldsymbol{\\psi}\\, \\partial \\boldsymbol{\\alpha}^\\top}\\bigg|_{\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\psi}},\\, \\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}}}.\n\\end{equation}\n%\nIn the G-MFRM, non-zero entries of $\\mathbf{C}$ arise from the contamination gating term $c_n \\cdot e_i$. The key entry is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\frac{\\partial^2 \\ell_3}{\\partial c_n\\, \\partial e_i} = \\sum_{j=1}^{J} \\frac{\\partial^2 \\ell_{nij}}{\\partial \\eta_{nij}^2} \\cdot c_n,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhich is proportional to $c_n$: models with zero contamination contribute no cross-derivative. Similarly, $\\partial^2 \\ell_3 / (\\partial \\theta_n\\, \\partial e_i)$ involves only indirect coupling through the shared item observations. The correction is therefore concentrated on the contamination parameters of models with $c_n > 0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Corrected variance.} The Murphy--Topel corrected variance is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:murphy_topel}\n\\mathbf{V}_{\\mathrm{MT}} = \\mathbf{V}_3 + \\mathbf{V}_3\\, \\mathbf{C}\\, \\mathbf{V}_2\\, \\mathbf{C}^\\top\\, \\mathbf{V}_3.\n\\end{equation}\n%\nThe correction term $\\mathbf{V}_3 \\mathbf{C} \\mathbf{V}_2 \\mathbf{C}^\\top \\mathbf{V}_3$ is positive semi-definite, so $\\mathbf{V}_{\\mathrm{MT}} \\succeq \\mathbf{V}_3$: the corrected variance is always at least as large as the naive variance. The inflation is concentrated on $c_n$ for contaminated models and is negligible for $\\theta_n$ when contamination is sparse, consistent with the empirical pattern in Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}.\n\n\\paragraph{Implementation.} We estimate $\\mathbf{V}_2$ from the Stage~2 bootstrap and compute $\\mathbf{C}$ via finite differences of the Stage~3 log-likelihood evaluated at perturbed $\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}}$ values. Corrected credible intervals are obtained by inflating Stage~3 posterior samples by $\\sqrt{\\mathrm{diag}(\\mathbf{V}_{\\mathrm{MT}}) / \\mathrm{diag}(\\mathbf{V}_3)}$.\n\n\n\\section{Ablation Study}\n\\label{app:ablation}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & $\\theta$-RMSE & $\\tau$ & $c_n$-RMSE \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull G-MFRM & 0.124 $\\pm$ 0.016 & 0.922 $\\pm$ 0.010 & 0.593 $\\pm$ 0.224 \\\\\n$-$ Judge severity ($d_j$) & 0.127 $\\pm$ 0.016 & 0.922 $\\pm$ 0.010 & 0.629 $\\pm$ 0.243 \\\\\nBinary gating & 0.137 $\\pm$ 0.016 & 0.909 $\\pm$ 0.014 & 0.833 $\\pm$ 0.226 \\\\\nStaged estimation & 0.254 $\\pm$ 0.045 & 0.839 $\\pm$ 0.035 & 1.382 $\\pm$ 0.164 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Ablation study on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds). Each row removes or modifies one component of the full G-MFRM. Staged estimation refers to fitting MFRM and contamination sequentially rather than jointly. All differences from Full G-MFRM are significant at $p < 0.001$ (Wilcoxon) except $-d_j$ on $\\theta$-RMSE ($p = 0.245$) and $\\tau$ ($p = 0.344$).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe ablation confirms that joint estimation is the primary contributor to G-MFRM's advantage: staged estimation increases $\\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\\times$ (from 0.124 to 0.254, $p < 0.001$) and $c_n$-RMSE by $2.3\\times$ (from 0.593 to 1.382, $p < 0.001$), with $\\tau$ dropping from 0.922 to 0.839. This degradation directly reflects the omitted variable bias characterized in Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}: when contamination and judge effects are estimated sequentially, the first-stage estimates absorb the unmodeled facet.\n\nReplacing continuous exposure $e_i \\in [0,1]$ with binary gating ($e_i \\in \\{0, 1\\}$) increases $c_n$-RMSE by 40\\% (from 0.593 to 0.833, $p < 0.001$), indicating that graded exposure modeling captures contamination magnitude more precisely than a binary flag. The effect on $\\theta$-RMSE is moderate ($+$10\\%, $p < 0.001$), suggesting that the binary approximation still captures the dominant contamination signal but loses precision on partially exposed items.\n\nRemoving judge severity $d_j$ does not significantly affect ability estimates ($\\theta$-RMSE $+$2.4\\%, $p = 0.245$; $\\tau$ identical, $p = 0.344$) but significantly degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6.1\\%, $p < 0.001$). This confirms that $d_j$ serves as a diagnostic component that improves contamination estimation precision rather than ranking accuracy: without explicit judge severity modeling, judge-induced score variation is partially absorbed into the contamination estimates, inflating $c_n$-RMSE. The $d_j$ profile also provides independent diagnostic value for judge calibration (e.g., Claude Opus $d_j = +0.60$ vs.\\ Mistral $-0.74$ in the real-data evaluation).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{theta_tau_boxplot}\n\\caption{Ranking accuracy (Kendall $\\tau$ with ground truth) across ablation variants. Full G-MFRM achieves the highest and most consistent $\\tau$; staged estimation shows the largest degradation and highest variance. Synthetic data: $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds.}\n\\label{fig:ablation_tau}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Posterior Predictive Check}\n\\label{app:ppc}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:ppc} shows the posterior predictive distribution from 1{,}000 posterior draws over 5{,}661 observations. The predicted and observed response distributions are well-aligned (predicted mean win rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$), with no systematic discrepancy across item difficulty or model ability levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ppc}\n\\caption{Posterior predictive check. Distribution of predicted (gray) and observed (black) response rates across all model--item--judge triplets. Mean predicted $= 0.593$, mean observed $= 0.599$.}\n\\label{fig:ppc}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{LOO Model Comparison}\n\\label{app:loo_comparison}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:model_comparison} visualizes the LOO-ELPD difference between G-MFRM and MFRM-only. G-MFRM is the reference model ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD} = 0$); MFRM-only shows a deficit of $-55.7$ (SE $= 9.4$), confirming that the contamination gating term substantially improves held-out predictive fit.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{model_comparison}\n\\caption{LOO-ELPD model comparison. G-MFRM (reference) versus MFRM-only. Error bars show $\\pm$1 SE. The $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$ strongly favors G-MFRM.}\n\\label{fig:model_comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:pareto_k} shows the Pareto $\\hat{k}$ diagnostic values for both models. All observations have $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming that the LOO approximation is reliable for both models.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{pareto_k_plot}\n\\caption{Pareto $\\hat{k}$ diagnostics for G-MFRM (left) and MFRM-only (right). All 5{,}661 observations fall below the $\\hat{k} = 0.7$ threshold, confirming reliable LOO estimates.}\n\\label{fig:pareto_k}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{OVB Validation Across Confounding Levels}\n\\label{app:ovb_validation}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho} (main text) shows that sequential estimator bias remains approximately constant across confounding levels ($\\rho$), confirming omission-driven rather than confounding-strength-dependent bias.\n\n\n\\section{Contamination Susceptibility Recovery}\n\\label{app:cn_recovery}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:cn_recovery} (main text) shows contamination susceptibility recovery from 50 Monte Carlo seeds.\n\n\n\\section{Error Propagation Details}\n\\label{app:error_prop_fig}\n\nThe error propagation figure is presented in the main text (Figure~\\ref{fig:error_prop}). See Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop} for numerical values.\n\n\n\\section{Self-Evaluation Robustness}\n\\label{app:self_eval}\n\nTwo models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---appear as both judge and target in our evaluation. Self-judging introduces a potential confound: a model may score its own responses more favorably. We examine the empirical magnitude of this effect and its impact on rankings.\n\nQwen2.5-72B achieves a perfect self-judging win rate of 1.000 (42/42 items), compared to a cross-judge mean win rate of 0.714 for the same model. LLaMA-3.3-70B shows a self-judging win rate of 0.912, compared to a cross-judge mean of 0.823. Both values are elevated relative to cross-judge baselines, consistent with same-family preference leakage \\citep{preference_leakage2026}.\n\nTo assess sensitivity, we re-fit G-MFRM excluding all 99 self-judging observations (Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B judging their own responses). The resulting ranking yields $\\tau = 0.857$, identical to the full-data G-MFRM point estimate, with a swap at ranks 2--3. Contamination susceptibility estimates $c_n$ shift by less than 0.15 for all models. This confirms that $d_j$ absorbs the bulk of self-evaluation bias and that rankings are robust to excluding self-judging pairs.\n\n\n\\section{Judge Severity Profiles}\n\\label{app:judge_severity}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:judge_severity} reports posterior estimates of judge severity $d_j$ for all nine judges. Positive values indicate stricter scoring (lower win rates); negative values indicate more lenient scoring. Proprietary models cluster at the strict end, while open-source models tend to be more lenient.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Judge} & $\\hat{d}_j$ & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\nClaude Opus 4.7 & $+0.60$ & [$+0.43$, $+0.76$] \\\\\nGPT-5.5 & $+0.53$ & [$+0.37$, $+0.70$] \\\\\nDeepSeek-V4-Pro & $+0.31$ & [$+0.07$, $+0.54$] \\\\\nClaude Sonnet 4.6 & $+0.14$ & [$-0.04$, $+0.31$] \\\\\nGemini-2.5-Pro & $-0.04$ & [$-0.27$, $+0.19$] \\\\\nQwen3-235B & $-0.04$ & [$-0.24$, $+0.16$] \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B & $-0.35$ & [$-0.64$, $-0.07$] \\\\\nLLaMA-3.3-70B & $-0.42$ & [$-0.66$, $-0.18$] \\\\\nMistral-Large-2 & $-0.74$ & [$-1.10$, $-0.37$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Judge severity estimates ($d_j$) for all nine judges. Positive = stricter scoring. The severity range spans 1.34 logits (Claude Opus to Mistral), comparable to $\\sim$1 standard deviation of model ability.}\n\\label{tab:judge_severity}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Judge Count Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:judge_count}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:judge_count} reports ranking accuracy as a function of the number of judges $k$, obtained by subsampling from the full 9-judge panel (10 random subsets per $k$; $k = 9$ uses the full panel). G-MFRM outperforms both raw averaging and sequential estimation at all judge counts. Rank agreement increases monotonically with $k$ ($\\tau = 0.771 \\to 0.857$), and variance contracts as additional judges improve $d_j$ and $c_n$ estimation. At $k \\geq 4$, G-MFRM exceeds the full-panel baseline $\\tau = 0.786$, suggesting that four diverse judges suffice for practical deployment.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & Raw Avg & Sequential & G-MFRM & $\\Delta\\tau$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n2 & $.755 \\pm .088$ & $.764 \\pm .096$ & $.771 \\pm .081$ & $+.017$ \\\\\n4 & $.779 \\pm .041$ & $.786 \\pm .048$ & $.800 \\pm .045$ & $+.021$ \\\\\n6 & $.800 \\pm .030$ & $.807 \\pm .048$ & $.821 \\pm .051$ & $+.021$ \\\\\n9 & $.786$ & $.786$ & $.857$ & $+.071$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Kendall $\\tau$ with Chatbot Arena rankings as a function of judge count $k$. $\\Delta\\tau$ = G-MFRM $-$ Raw Average. Values for $k < 9$ are mean $\\pm$ std over 10 random judge subsets.}\n\\label{tab:judge_count}\n\\end{table}\n\nLeave-one-judge-out analysis reveals that three judges---Claude Opus 4.7 ($d_j = +0.60$), GPT-5.5 ($+0.53$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($-0.42$)---are individually necessary for the ranking improvement: removing any one of these reduces G-MFRM $\\tau$ to the baseline ($0.786$), while removing any of the other six judges preserves $\\tau = 0.857$. These three judges span the severity spectrum (the two strictest and the second-most lenient), suggesting that contamination$\\times$judge interaction identification requires sufficient severity diversity. This provides practical guidance: judge panels should include models with diverse scoring tendencies rather than homogeneous judges.\n\nG-MFRM requires $\\geq 4$ MCMC chains for reliable contamination estimation; reduced chain counts attenuate $c_n$ estimates toward the prior mean, compressing inter-model differences.\n\n\n\\section{Stage~2 Weight Robustness}\n\\label{app:weight_robustness}\n\nAll four weight configurations produce identical model rankings and near-perfect exposure score agreement (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.998$; Table~\\ref{tab:weight_robustness}), confirming that the default 75/25 split is not a sensitive hyperparameter choice.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Weight Config} & \\textbf{Exposure} $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n50 / 50 & 0.998 \\\\\n75 / 25 (default) & 1.000 \\\\\n90 / 10 & 0.999 \\\\\n100 / 0 & 0.998 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Stage~2 weight robustness. Weight Config denotes the percentage assigned to reference model anchoring vs.\\ cross-model agreement. All configurations produce identical model rankings. Exposure $\\rho$ is the Spearman correlation of per-item $e_i$ scores relative to the default 75/25 configuration.}\n\\label{tab:weight_robustness}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Power Analysis}\n\\label{app:power}\n\nThe observed effect size in the real-data evaluation is $\\Delta\\tau = 0.071$ (G-MFRM $\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ baseline $\\tau = 0.786$). We conduct a permutation-based power analysis to determine the sample sizes required to detect this effect at $\\alpha = 0.05$.\n\nWe simulate ranking comparisons under the alternative hypothesis $H_1\\!: \\Delta\\tau = 0.071$ by bootstrapping from the observed posterior distribution of G-MFRM ability estimates and recomputing $\\tau$ against ground truth at varying model counts $N$. For each $N$, we draw 10{,}000 permutation replicates and compute the rejection rate at $\\alpha = 0.05$ (two-sided).\n\nAt $N = 8$ (our evaluation), power is approximately 50\\%, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result ($\\Delta\\tau$ 95\\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$). Achieving 80\\% power requires $N \\approx 17$ models, and 95\\% power requires $N \\approx 25$. These estimates inform the scope of future evaluations: detecting ranking improvements of the magnitude observed here requires at minimum doubling the number of target models.\n\n\n\\section{Per-Model Estimates}\n\\label{app:raw_scores}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:raw_scores} reports G-MFRM posterior estimates for all eight target models. All seven baselines produce identical rankings (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), with ability scores differing by $< 0.03$ logits across methods; G-MFRM is the only method that alters the ranking, swapping gpt-4o-mini and Qwen2.5-72B at ranks 2--3.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & $\\hat{\\theta}$ [95\\% CI] & $\\hat{c}_n$ [95\\% CI] & \\textbf{Base} & \\textbf{G-MFRM} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-4o & $+2.00$ [$1.70$, $2.30$] & $0.88$ [$0.05$, $2.20$] & 1 & 1 \\\\\nGPT-4o-mini & $+1.40$ [$1.10$, $1.70$] & $0.93$ [$0.06$, $2.20$] & 3 & 2 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B & $+1.20$ [$0.85$, $1.50$] & $3.00$ [$1.40$, $4.60$] & 2 & 3 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.3-70B & $+0.54$ [$0.23$, $0.85$] & $2.50$ [$1.10$, $3.90$] & 4 & 4 \\\\\nQwen3-8B & $+0.51$ [$0.22$, $0.79$] & $1.20$ [$0.14$, $2.50$] & 5 & 5 \\\\\nGemma-2-9B & $-0.50$ [$-0.79$, $-0.21$] & $1.70$ [$0.36$, $2.90$] & 6 & 6 \\\\\nGPT-4-0613 & $-1.90$ [$-2.20$, $-1.60$] & $4.20$ [$3.10$, $5.30$] & 7 & 7 \\\\\nGPT-3.5-Turbo & $-3.30$ [$-3.60$, $-3.00$] & $5.60$ [$4.40$, $6.80$] & 8 & 8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{G-MFRM posterior estimates for ability ($\\hat{\\theta}$) and contamination susceptibility ($\\hat{c}_n$) with approximate 95\\% credible intervals (empirical coverage ${\\sim}$71\\%; see Limitations). Point estimates and rank orderings are the primary results. ``Base'' and ``G-MFRM'' columns show model rankings; the only rank change is the swap at positions 2--3.}\n\\label{tab:raw_scores}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Contamination Bias Absorption}\n\\label{app:bias_absorption}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:bias_absorption} compares per-model ability estimates from MFRM-only and G-MFRM. The difference $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{G-MFRM}}$ is perfectly rank-correlated with contamination susceptibility $c_n$ (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$), providing direct empirical evidence that MFRM-only absorbs the omitted contamination term into ability estimates.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & $\\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MFRM}}$ & $\\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{G-MFRM}}$ & $\\Delta\\theta$ & $\\hat{c}_n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-3.5-Turbo & $-2.487$ & $-3.274$ & $+0.786$ & $5.587$ \\\\\nGPT-4-0613 & $-1.446$ & $-1.877$ & $+0.431$ & $4.168$ \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B & $+1.213$ & $+1.178$ & $+0.035$ & $2.961$ \\\\\nLLaMA-3.3-70B & $+0.518$ & $+0.539$ & $-0.022$ & $2.478$ \\\\\nGemma-2-9B & $-0.661$ & $-0.500$ & $-0.161$ & $1.654$ \\\\\nQwen3-8B & $+0.208$ & $+0.513$ & $-0.305$ & $1.178$ \\\\\nGPT-4o-mini & $+1.064$ & $+1.432$ & $-0.368$ & $0.934$ \\\\\nGPT-4o & $+1.591$ & $+1.988$ & $-0.397$ & $0.880$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Contamination bias absorption in MFRM-only estimation. $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{G-MFRM}}$. Models with high contamination susceptibility ($c_n$) have inflated ability estimates under MFRM-only; models with low $c_n$ are relatively deflated. The perfect rank correlation (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$) between $\\Delta\\theta$ and $c_n$ empirically confirms the omitted variable bias predicted in Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}.}\n\\label{tab:bias_absorption}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Synthetic Calibration at Matched Scale}\n\\label{app:synthetic_calibration}\n\nWe generate synthetic data at matched scale ($N = 8$ models, $I = 176$ items, $J = 9$ judges) with known ground-truth parameters across 20 random seeds. Joint G-MFRM consistently outperforms sequential estimation: $\\theta$-RMSE is halved ($0.083$ vs.\\ $0.166$), and ranking agreement improves by 7.2 percentage points (mean $\\tau = 0.954$ vs.\\ $0.882$). Across 20 seeds, joint estimation improves or matches sequential in all cases (10 wins, 0 losses, 10 ties; Wilcoxon signed-rank $p < 0.002$). The synthetic effect size ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.072$) closely matches the observed real-data effect ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$). All 40 MCMC fits converge ($\\hat{R} = 1.0$, ESS $\\geq 1{,}678$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Sequential} & \\textbf{Joint G-MFRM} & \\textbf{Improv.} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\theta$ RMSE & $0.166 \\pm 0.050$ & $0.083 \\pm 0.024$ & $-50.3\\%$ \\\\\n$\\theta$ $\\tau$ & $0.882 \\pm 0.112$ & $0.954 \\pm 0.048$ & $+7.2$pp \\\\\n$c_n$ RMSE & $1.399 \\pm 0.358$ & $0.552 \\pm 0.328$ & $-60.5\\%$ \\\\\n$c_n$ Pearson & $0.850 \\pm 0.141$ & $0.866 \\pm 0.151$ & $+1.9$\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Synthetic calibration at $N = 8$ ($I = 176$, $J = 9$, 20 seeds). Joint G-MFRM halves $\\theta$-RMSE and reduces $c_n$-RMSE by 61\\% relative to sequential estimation.}\n\\label{tab:synthetic_calibration}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt $N = 20$ (20 seeds), the ranking improvement becomes individually significant: joint G-MFRM achieves $\\Delta\\tau = +0.052$ over sequential estimation (Wilcoxon $p = 1.81 \\times 10^{-4}$, 18 wins / 1 loss / 1 tie), directly validating the power analysis prediction that $N \\approx 17$ suffices for 80\\% power. The effect size is stable across scales ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.072$ at $N = 8$, $+0.052$ at $N = 20$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Sequential} & \\textbf{Joint G-MFRM} & \\textbf{Improv.} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\theta$ RMSE & $0.171 \\pm 0.049$ & $0.094 \\pm 0.019$ & $-45.2\\%$ \\\\\n$\\theta$ $\\tau$ & $0.891 \\pm 0.038$ & $0.943 \\pm 0.036$ & $+5.2$pp \\\\\n$c_n$ RMSE & $1.375 \\pm 0.278$ & $0.626 \\pm 0.263$ & $-54.5\\%$ \\\\\n$c_n$ Pearson & $0.817 \\pm 0.096$ & $0.842 \\pm 0.106$ & $+2.5\\%$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Synthetic calibration at $N = 20$ ($I = 176$, $J = 9$, 20 seeds). Ranking improvement is individually significant (Wilcoxon $p = 1.81 \\times 10^{-4}$), validating the power analysis prediction.}\n\\label{tab:synthetic_calibration_n20}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Prior Specification Robustness}\n\\label{app:prior_robustness}\n\nWe compare posterior estimates under two contamination priors: Horseshoe \\citep{piironen2017horseshoe} and hierarchical HalfNormal (Eq.~\\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Table~\\ref{tab:prior_robustness} reports $\\hat{c}_n$ and $\\hat{\\theta}$ posterior means for all eight target models under both specifications.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & $\\hat{c}_n$ (HS) & $\\hat{c}_n$ (HN) & $\\hat{\\theta}$ (HS) & $\\hat{\\theta}$ (HN) \\\\\n\\midrule\nGemma-2-9B & 1.61 & 1.65 & $-0.49$ & $-0.50$ \\\\\nGPT-3.5-Turbo & 5.69 & 5.57 & $-3.30$ & $-3.27$ \\\\\nGPT-4-0613 & 4.25 & 4.15 & $-1.89$ & $-1.88$ \\\\\nGPT-4o & 0.79 & 0.88 & $+2.01$ & $+1.99$ \\\\\nGPT-4o-mini & 0.84 & 0.92 & $+1.45$ & $+1.43$ \\\\\nLLaMA-3.3-70B & 2.50 & 2.46 & $+0.53$ & $+0.54$ \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B & 3.02 & 2.94 & $+1.17$ & $+1.18$ \\\\\nQwen3-8B & 1.11 & 1.15 & $+0.53$ & $+0.52$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Posterior estimates under Horseshoe (HS) and HalfNormal (HN) contamination priors. Both priors produce virtually identical estimates: Pearson $r = 1.000$ for $\\hat{\\theta}$ and $r = 0.9999$ for $\\hat{c}_n$.}\n\\label{tab:prior_robustness}\n\\end{table}\n\nPosterior estimates are virtually identical across the two prior specifications (Pearson $r = 1.000$ for $\\hat{\\theta}$, $r > 0.999$ for $\\hat{c}_n$), indicating that results are not sensitive to the choice of contamination prior.\n\n\n\\section{Item-Level Fit Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:item_diagnostics}\n\nWe report item-level infit and outfit MNSQ statistics for all 176 items. Items with MNSQ outside $[0.5, 1.5]$ are flagged as potentially misfitting.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Statistic} & \\textbf{Mean} & \\textbf{SD} & \\textbf{Median} & \\textbf{Min} & \\textbf{Max} \\\\\n\\midrule\nInfit MNSQ & 0.78 & 0.41 & 0.76 & 0.05 & 1.95 \\\\\nOutfit MNSQ & 0.73 & 0.57 & 0.58 & 0.04 & 3.17 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Item-level fit statistics across 176 items.}\n\\label{tab:item_fit_summary}\n\\end{table}\n\nOf the 176 items, 92 are flagged: 18 overfit (MNSQ $> 1.5$) and 74 underfit (MNSQ $< 0.5$). The majority of flagged items are underfit, reflecting sparse low-observation items where the model's predicted variance exceeds the observed variance---a consequence of limited data per item rather than a structural model deficiency. The 18 overfit items exhibit more erratic response patterns than the model predicts. Importantly, no model-level or judge-level facets are flagged (all MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$), confirming that the G-MFRM captures the systematic structure in model ability, judge severity, and contamination.\n\n\n\\section{CI Coverage Analysis}\n\\label{app:ci_coverage}\n\nWe assess empirical coverage of 95\\% posterior credible intervals across synthetic replications. At $N=8$ (49 seeds), ability parameter $\\theta$ achieves 90.1\\% coverage, approaching the nominal level, while contamination susceptibility $c_n$ achieves only 67.9\\%. At $N=50$ (50 seeds; exp-009), $c_n$ coverage improves to 71\\%. The coverage gap between $\\theta$ and $c_n$ reflects the multiplicative coupling $c_n \\cdot e_i$: contamination parameters enter the likelihood through an interaction term, making posterior inference more sensitive to exposure estimation noise and the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint at $c_n = 0$. These results support treating $c_n$ point estimates and rank orderings as reliable diagnostics, while noting that $c_n$ credible intervals are systematically too narrow.\n\n\\section{Use of AI Assistants}\n\\label{app:ai_assistants}\n\nWe used Claude (Anthropic) as an AI coding and writing assistant\nthroughout this work. Specifically:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Code:} drafting experiment scripts, data-processing\n    pipelines, and analysis utilities; debugging and refactoring.\n    \\item \\textbf{Writing:} drafting and revising paper sections,\n    polishing prose, and reformatting LaTeX.\n    \\item \\textbf{Literature:} summarizing related papers to inform\n    the related-work section.\n\\end{itemize}\n",
      "numLines": 497,

      "totalLines": 497
    }

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}
5	LLM-as-a-judge evaluations exhibit position bias \citep{wang2024positionbias}, self-preference \citep{panickssery2024selffavor}, and other cognitive biases \citep{koo2024cobbler, ye2024justice, bavaresco2025judgebench}; see also \citet{li2024splitmerge} and \citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty}. On the correction side, CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias via round-robin assignment, and UDA \citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\%. All of these methods model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination effects into ability estimates (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Benchmark Contamination Detection and Avoidance.}
8	Detection methods include performance-based tests \citep{constat2024}, unified contamination metrics \citep{dcr2025}, token-level log-probability probes \citep{logprober2024, mink2024}, and guided prompting \citep{golchin2024timetravel, oren2024proving}; see also \citet{deng2024contamination, sainz2023contamination}. \citet{preference_leakage2026} identify judge-side preference leakage, complementary to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus. Avoidance strategies include dynamic benchmarks \citep{livebench2025}, private evaluation \citep{truce2024}, and subset selection \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks}. G-MFRM instead corrects for residual contamination while simultaneously modeling judge effects.
9	
10	\paragraph{Item Response Theory (IRT) and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}
11	IRT has been applied to NLP leaderboards for ranking and benchmark analysis \citep{lalor2019irt, rodriguez2021irt, vania2021irt}. The Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) \citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} extends IRT to multi-rater settings; \citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply it to LLM judge panels, and \citet{uto2020generalized} and \citet{choi2026irt_judge} propose Bayesian variants; see also \citet{zhou2025psnirt}. The Doubly-Gated IRT (DG-IRT) model of \citet{shu2013dgirt} is the closest antecedent, introducing binary gating for item preknowledge; G-MFRM extends this to continuous exposure, multi-rater designs, and formal identifiability analysis (Section~\ref{sec:model_spec}).
12	
```

docs/paper/experiments.tex:18:\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated via a two-source pipeline (\S\ref{sec:staged}): reference model anchoring with gpt-4-0314 as anchor (75\% weight) and cross-model agreement (25\% weight). The pipeline is robust to weight configuration (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.998$ across all configurations tested).
docs/paper/experiments.tex:20:\paragraph{MCMC.} We use NumPyro's NUTS sampler with 4{,}000 warmup and 4{,}000 sampling iterations across 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). The contamination prior follows the hierarchical HalfNormal specification (Eq.~\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\hat{R}$ \citep{vehtari2021rhat} with conventional $\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS $> 100$ thresholds \citep{gelman2013bda}.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:25:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/experiments.tex:54:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/experiments.tex:58:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/experiments.tex:62:\paragraph{Ranking accuracy.} The $\Delta\tau = +0.071$ improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\% CI crosses zero; post-hoc power ${\sim}$50\%). Power analysis indicates $N \approx 17$ for 80\% power (Appendix~\ref{app:power}), validated at $N = 20$ where the improvement reaches significance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}).
docs/paper/experiments.tex:64:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/experiments.tex:69:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/method.tex:11:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/method.tex:16:G-MFRM extends the standard MFRM \citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} by adding a multiplicative contamination gating term to the linear predictor (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}):
docs/paper/method.tex:23:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/method.tex:27:G-MFRM extends the Doubly-Gated IRT model of \citet{shu2013dgirt} from binary gating to continuous exposure and multi-rater designs, and contributes the first formal identifiability conditions for gated IRT parameters (Propositions~\ref{prop:no_new}--\ref{prop:exposure_variation}, Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}) and error propagation bounds for staged estimation (\S\ref{sec:error_prop}).
docs/paper/method.tex:32:Joint estimation of all parameters in Eq.~\ref{eq:gmfrm}, including $e_i$, would require identifying the bilinear product $c_n \cdot e_i$ from response data alone. This creates a fundamental non-identifiability (Proposition~\ref{prop:exposure_variation}). We resolve this through a three-stage procedure that fixes exposure externally before estimating the contamination parameters.
docs/paper/method.tex:34:\paragraph{Stage 1: Standard MFRM.} We fit the standard MFRM (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}) via MCMC (NUTS) to obtain initial estimates $(\hat{\theta}, \hat{b}, \hat{d})$. These serve as warm-start values for the sampler in Stage~3 and improve convergence.
docs/paper/method.tex:36:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/method.tex:38:\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed, we sample $(\theta_n, b_i, d_j, c_n)$ jointly via the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) \citep{betancourt2015}. The priors are as specified in \S\ref{sec:model_spec}. The hierarchical contamination prior decomposes as
docs/paper/method.tex:48:Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\hat{R}$ \citep{vehtari2021rhat} and bulk effective sample size (ESS), with thresholds $\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS$_{\mathrm{bulk}} > 100$ following standard practice \citep{gelman2013bda}.\footnote{Synthetic validation with $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$ yields satisfactory convergence across all parameters (exp-012). Full diagnostic tables are reported in Section~\ref{sec:experiments}.}
docs/paper/method.tex:77:Proofs of Propositions~\ref{prop:no_new} and~\ref{prop:exposure_variation} are given in Appendix~\ref{app:prop_proofs}.
docs/paper/method.tex:84:The proof (Appendix~\ref{app:proof}) follows \citet{rothenberg1971}, establishing Fisher information non-singularity via the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality on the $(\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix and a Schur complement for the remaining blocks.
docs/paper/method.tex:98:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/method.tex:100:Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} and Figure~\ref{fig:error_prop} show empirical degradation: below $\sigma = 0.2$, $\theta$-RMSE increases by 5.9\% and $c_n$-RMSE by 7.9\%; beyond the threshold ($\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes severe. Contamination estimates are more sensitive because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$.
docs/paper/method.tex:119:For honest credible intervals, we apply the Murphy--Topel variance correction \citep{murphy1985} to account for generated-regressor uncertainty (Appendix~\ref{app:murphy_topel}).
docs/paper/limitations.tex:3:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/limitations.tex:5:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/background.tex:34:When a practitioner fits the standard MFRM (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}) to data generated by Eq.~\ref{eq:full}, the contamination term is absent from the estimated model. Its influence does not vanish but is absorbed into the estimated parameters $\tilde{\theta}_n$, $\tilde{b}_i$, and $\tilde{d}_j$, which distorts all downstream inferences.
docs/paper/background.tex:45:where $\gamma_n$ depends on the projection of the exposure vector $(e_i)$ onto the design matrix of the short model (explicit form in Appendix~\ref{app:gamma}). The bias is non-zero whenever $\mathrm{Cov}(d_j,\, c_n \cdot e_i) \neq 0$. This captures the confounding component; in the nonlinear MFRM, a specification bias from omitting $c_n \cdot e_i$ persists even at zero covariance (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).

docs/paper/appendix.tex:8:\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:exposure_variation}]

docs/paper/appendix.tex:16:We prove that the G-MFRM is locally identifiable under the conditions of Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}. Following \citet{rothenberg1971}, a parametric model is locally identifiable at the true parameter vector $\boldsymbol{\psi}_0$ if and only if the Fisher information matrix $\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_0)$ is non-singular.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:79:[Omitted long matching line]

docs/paper/appendix.tex:94:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/appendix.tex:137:The correction term $\mathbf{V}_3 \mathbf{C} \mathbf{V}_2 \mathbf{C}^\top \mathbf{V}_3$ is positive semi-definite, so $\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{MT}} \succeq \mathbf{V}_3$: the corrected variance is always at least as large as the naive variance. The inflation is concentrated on $c_n$ for contaminated models and is negligible for $\theta_n$ when contamination is sparse, consistent with the empirical pattern in Table~\ref{tab:error_prop}.

docs/paper/appendix.tex:180:Figure~\ref{fig:ppc} shows the posterior predictive distribution from 1{,}000 posterior draws over 5{,}661 observations. The predicted and observed response distributions are well-aligned (predicted mean win rate $0.593$ vs.\ observed $0.599$), with no systematic discrepancy across item difficulty or model ability levels.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:193:Figure~\ref{fig:model_comparison} visualizes the LOO-ELPD difference between G-MFRM and MFRM-only. G-MFRM is the reference model ($\Delta\mathrm{ELPD} = 0$); MFRM-only shows a deficit of $-55.7$ (SE $= 9.4$), confirming that the contamination gating term substantially improves held-out predictive fit.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:202:Figure~\ref{fig:pareto_k} shows the Pareto $\hat{k}$ diagnostic values for both models. All observations have $\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming that the LOO approximation is reliable for both models.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:215:Figure~\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho} (main text) shows that sequential estimator bias remains approximately constant across confounding levels ($\rho$), confirming omission-driven rather than confounding-strength-dependent bias.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:221:Figure~\ref{fig:cn_recovery} (main text) shows contamination susceptibility recovery from 50 Monte Carlo seeds.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:227:The error propagation figure is presented in the main text (Figure~\ref{fig:error_prop}). See Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} for numerical values.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:243:Table~\ref{tab:judge_severity} reports posterior estimates of judge severity $d_j$ for all nine judges. Positive values indicate stricter scoring (lower win rates); negative values indicate more lenient scoring. Proprietary models cluster at the strict end, while open-source models tend to be more lenient.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:271:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/appendix.tex:298:All four weight configurations produce identical model rankings and near-perfect exposure score agreement (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.998$; Table~\ref{tab:weight_robustness}), confirming that the default 75/25 split is not a sensitive hyperparameter choice.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:331:Table~\ref{tab:raw_scores} reports G-MFRM posterior estimates for all eight target models. All seven baselines produce identical rankings (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), with ability scores differing by $< 0.03$ logits across methods; G-MFRM is the only method that alters the ranking, swapping gpt-4o-mini and Qwen2.5-72B at ranks 2--3.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:360:Table~\ref{tab:bias_absorption} compares per-model ability estimates from MFRM-only and G-MFRM. The difference $\Delta\theta = \hat{\theta}_{\text{MFRM}} - \hat{\theta}_{\text{G-MFRM}}$ is perfectly rank-correlated with contamination susceptibility $c_n$ (Spearman $\rho = 1.0$), providing direct empirical evidence that MFRM-only absorbs the omitted contamination term into ability estimates.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:380:\caption{Contamination bias absorption in MFRM-only estimation. $\Delta\theta = \hat{\theta}_{\text{MFRM}} - \hat{\theta}_{\text{G-MFRM}}$. Models with high contamination susceptibility ($c_n$) have inflated ability estimates under MFRM-only; models with low $c_n$ are relatively deflated. The perfect rank correlation (Spearman $\rho = 1.0$) between $\Delta\theta$ and $c_n$ empirically confirms the omitted variable bias predicted in Section~\ref{sec:confounding}.}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:430:We compare posterior estimates under two contamination priors: Horseshoe \citep{piironen2017horseshoe} and hierarchical HalfNormal (Eq.~\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Table~\ref{tab:prior_robustness} reports $\hat{c}_n$ and $\hat{\theta}$ posterior means for all eight target models under both specifications.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:13:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/introduction.tex:15:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/introduction.tex:17:    \item The G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees and quantified error propagation bounds for staged estimation (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
docs/paper/introduction.tex:18:    \item Synthetic validation confirming 51.2\% RMSE reduction over sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$; Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
docs/paper/introduction.tex:19:    \item On Arena-Hard, the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity within a measurement-theoretic framework (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{forest_dj}\n\\caption{Judge severity estimates ($d_j$) for all nine judges with 94\\% credible intervals. Positive values indicate stricter scoring. Proprietary judges (Claude Opus, GPT-5.5) cluster at the strict end; open-source judges (Mistral, LLaMA) are more lenient. The severity range spans 1.34 logits.}\n\\label{fig:judge_severity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \\citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 316 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. After filtering for coverage, the evaluation set comprises 176 items in a connected incomplete design (XX{,}XXX observations; XX.X\\% fill rate of the full $N \\times I \\times J$ tensor). Seventeen target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated: the original 8 models (GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, Qwen2.5-72B, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen3-8B, Gemma-2-9B, GPT-4-0613, GPT-3.5-Turbo) plus 9 additional models (Claude-3.5-Sonnet, GPT-4-Turbo, LLaMA-3.1-70B, Gemma-2-27B, Claude-3-Opus, LLaMA-3-70B, Mixtral-8x22B, Claude-3-Haiku, LLaMA-3-8B). Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}.\n\n\\paragraph{Judges.} Nine LLM judges score model responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.7, Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Mistral-Large-2, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Seven judges cover all 17 target models; two judges (GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7) cover only the original 8 models, yielding an asymmetric connected design---Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only connectivity, not full crossing. Position swapping controls for position bias and doubles the effective observation count. Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs systematic self-evaluation bias, and excluding self-judging observations leaves rankings unchanged ($\\tau = 0.857$; Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated via a two-source pipeline (\\S\\ref{sec:staged}): reference model anchoring with gpt-4-0314 as anchor (75\\% weight) and cross-model agreement (25\\% weight). The pipeline is robust to weight configuration (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.998$ across all configurations tested).\n\n\\paragraph{MCMC.} We use NumPyro's NUTS sampler with 4{,}000 warmup and 4{,}000 sampling iterations across 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). The contamination prior follows the hierarchical HalfNormal specification (Eq.~\\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat} with conventional $\\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS $> 100$ thresholds \\citep{gelman2013bda}.\n\n\\subsection{Baselines and Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nWe compare G-MFRM against seven methods that address judge bias or contamination separately: (1) Raw Average, (2) MFRM-only (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}), (3) Sequential pipeline (MFRM then contamination adjustment), (4) CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, (5) CyclicJudge+discard (exposed items removed), (6) DCR Factor \\citep{dcr2025}, and (7) Preference Leakage calibration \\citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\\tau$ and Spearman $\\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{8 models}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{17 models}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\tau$ & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nRaw Average & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.838 & 0.949 \\\\\nMFRM-only & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\\\\nSequential & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\\\\nCyclicJudge & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.838 & 0.949 \\\\\nCyclicJudge+discard & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.824 & 0.934 \\\\\nDCR Factor & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.824 & 0.946 \\\\\nPref.\\ Leakage & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.838 & 0.949 \\\\\n\\midrule\nG-MFRM (ours) & \\textbf{0.857} & \\textbf{0.952} & \\textbf{TBD} & \\textbf{TBD} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Correlation with Chatbot Arena human rankings on Arena-Hard (9 judges). All seven baselines produce identical rankings at $N = 8$ ($\\tau = 0.786$). G-MFRM yields nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($+$7.1pp; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero). Right columns report the 17-model evaluation (TBD).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig6_cn_profile}\n\\caption{Per-model contamination diagnostics from G-MFRM. (a)~Posterior estimates of contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ with 95\\% credible intervals, ordered by magnitude. Older models (GPT-3.5-Turbo, GPT-4-0613) show 4--6$\\times$ higher susceptibility than recent models (GPT-4o). (b)~Ability bias absorbed by MFRM-only estimation: $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{G\\text{-}MFRM}}$. The perfect rank correlation (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$) confirms that contamination inflates MFRM ability estimates for high-$c_n$ models and deflates them for low-$c_n$ models, as predicted by the OVB analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).}\n\\label{fig:cn_profile}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nAll seven baselines produce identical rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$, $\\rho = 0.905$; raw ability scores differ by $< 0.03$ logits, Table~\\ref{tab:raw_scores}). G-MFRM achieves nominally higher rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$, $\\rho = 0.952$), but bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ 95\\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$ includes zero at $N = 8$, though $P(\\Delta\\tau \\geq 0) = 0.927$. On synthetic data ($N = 50$), the improvement is significant ($\\tau = 0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming the effect is real but below detection at this sample size.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictive fit.} LOO cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, SE $= 9.4$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 and all Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$.\n\n\\paragraph{Ranking accuracy.} The $\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$ improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI crosses zero; post-hoc power ${\\sim}$50\\%). Power analysis indicates $N \\approx 17$ for 80\\% power (Appendix~\\ref{app:power}), validated at $N = 20$ where the improvement reaches significance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}).\n\nAll parameters achieve $\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ with ESS $\\geq 174$ and zero divergent transitions; model- and judge-level fit statistics fall within acceptable ranges (MNSQ $\\in [0.63, 1.07]$; full diagnostics in Appendices~\\ref{app:item_diagnostics} and~\\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age (Figure~\\ref{fig:cn_profile}a): the four oldest models show $\\hat{c}_n > 1.0$ (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $5.60$; GPT-4-0613: $4.20$; Qwen2.5-72B: $3.00$; LLaMA-3.3-70B: $2.50$), while recent models show low contamination (GPT-4o: $0.88$; GPT-4o-mini: $0.93$). MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (Figure~\\ref{fig:cn_profile}b; Table~\\ref{tab:bias_absorption}). Results are robust to prior specification and judge panel size ($k \\geq 4$ suffices; Appendices~\\ref{app:prior_robustness} and~\\ref{app:judge_count}).\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}\n\\label{sec:error_prop_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} validates the theoretical prediction (\\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}): below $\\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial. The severity range spans 1.34 logits.}\n\\label{fig:judge_severity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \\citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 316 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. After filtering for coverage, the evaluation set comprises 176 items in a connected incomplete design (36{,}231 observations; 66.0\\% fill rate of the full $N \\times I \\times J$ tensor). Seventeen target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated: the original 8 models (GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, Qwen2.5-72B, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen3-8B, Gemma-2-9B, GPT-4-0613, GPT-3.5-Turbo) plus 9 additional models (Claude-3.5-Sonnet, GPT-4-Turbo, LLaMA-3.1-70B, Gemma-2-27B, Claude-3-Opus, LLaMA-3-70B, Mixtral-8x22B, Claude-3-Haiku, LLaMA-3-8B). Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}.\n\n\\paragraph{Judges.} Nine LLM judges score model responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.7, Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Mistral-Large-2, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Seven judges cover all 17 target models; two judges (GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7) cover only the original 8 models, yielding an asymmetric connected design---Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only connectivity, not full crossing. Position swapping controls for position bias and doubles the effective observation count. Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs systematic self-evaluation bias, and excluding self-judging observations leaves rankings unchanged ($\\tau = 0.857$; Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated via a two-source pipeline (\\S\\ref{sec:staged}): reference model anchoring with gpt-4-0314 as anchor (75\\% weight) and cross-model agreement (25\\% weight). The pipeline is robust to weight configuration (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.998$ across all configurations tested).\n\n\\paragraph{MCMC.} We use NumPyro's NUTS sampler with 4{,}000 warmup and 4{,}000 sampling iterations across 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). The contamination prior follows the hierarchical HalfNormal specification (Eq.~\\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat} with conventional $\\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS $> 100$ thresholds \\citep{gelman2013bda}.\n\n\\subsection{Baselines and Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nWe compare G-MFRM against seven methods that address judge bias or contamination separately: (1) Raw Average, (2) MFRM-only (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}), (3) Sequential pipeline (MFRM then contamination adjustment), (4) CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, (5) CyclicJudge+discard (exposed items removed), (6) DCR Factor \\citep{dcr2025}, and (7) Preference Leakage calibration \\citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\\tau$ and Spearman $\\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{8 models}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{17 models}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\tau$ & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nRaw Average & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.838 & 0.949 \\\\\nMFRM-only & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\\\\nSequential & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\\\\nCyclicJudge & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.838 & 0.949 \\\\\nCyclicJudge+discard & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.824 & 0.934 \\\\\nDCR Factor & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.824 & 0.946 \\\\\nPref.\\ Leakage & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.838 & 0.949 \\\\\n\\midrule\nG-MFRM (ours) & \\textbf{0.857} & \\textbf{0.952} & \\textbf{TBD} & \\textbf{TBD} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Correlation with Chatbot Arena human rankings on Arena-Hard (9 judges). Older models (GPT-3.5-Turbo, GPT-4-0613) show 4--6$\\times$ higher susceptibility than recent models (GPT-4o).     "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe introduced G-MFRM, which jointly estimates model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility by formalizing their confounding as omitted variable bias. On Arena-Hard, G-MFRM produces the first joint contamination--severity diagnostic within a measurement-theoretic framework, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$; not significant at $N = 8$); well-powered validation requires $N \\geq 20$ (Limitations). Treating judge bias and contamination as separable leads to systematic estimation errors; future LLM evaluation frameworks should model these facets jointly.\n",

    "oldString": "recovering contamination-corrected ability $\\tilde{\\theta}_n$",
    "newString": "recovering contamination-corrected ability $\\hat{\\theta}_n$",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge Language Model (LLM) evaluation increasingly relies on LLM-as-a-judge protocols, where one or more LLM judges score model responses on structured benchmarks \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}. Two well-documented sources of distortion affect these evaluations: \\emph{judge bias}, where different judges assign systematically different scores to the same response \\citep{wang2024positionbias, ye2024justice, panickssery2024selffavor}, and \\emph{benchmark contamination}, where models achieve inflated scores on items encountered during training \\citep{dcr2025, mink2024}. Existing methods address these distortions independently: judge bias corrections calibrate inter-judge differences without modeling contamination \\citep{cyclicjudge2026, uda2026}, while contamination detection tools adjust for memorization without accounting for judge effects \\citep{constat2024, dcr2025, logprober2024}. A practitioner who needs both corrections must apply them sequentially, but this produces unreliable estimates. Accurate ability ranking is essential for model selection, safety evaluation, and benchmark design; systematic distortion undermines all three.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_framework_v2}\n\\caption{(a)~Sequential pipelines address judge bias and contamination independently; omitted variable bias confounds the unmodeled facet into ability estimates. (b)~G-MFRM three-stage joint calibration: Stage~1 fits a standard MFRM for initial estimates $(\\hat{\\theta}, \\hat{b}, \\hat{d})$; Stage~2 estimates per-item exposure $e_i$ from external evidence (reference model anchoring, cross-model agreement, and optionally LogProber); Stage~3 performs joint MCMC inference with $e_i$ fixed, recovering contamination-corrected ability $\\tilde{\\theta}_n$, contamination susceptibility $c_n$, and judge severity $d_j$.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe formalize this failure as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem \\citep{angrist2009mostly}. Within the Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) framework \\citep{linacre1989mfrm}, the complete data-generating process includes both judge severity $d_j$ and a contamination interaction $c_n \\cdot e_i$. Fitting a model that omits either facet produces biased parameter estimates: a bias-first pipeline absorbs contamination into ability estimates, while a contamination-first pipeline misattributes judge severity as contamination signal. The bias is directional---contaminated models are systematically overestimated---and persists regardless of the ordering (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\nWe propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly estimates model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility in a single measurement framework. G-MFRM extends MFRM with a multiplicative gating term $c_n \\cdot e_i$, inspired by Doubly-Gated IRT \\citep{shu2013dgirt}, and employs a three-stage calibration procedure with externally anchored per-item exposure indicators (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). We provide formal identifiability guarantees (Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and error propagation bounds for the staged estimation. In synthetic experiments ($N = 50$), G-MFRM reduces ability root mean squared error (RMSE) by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$). On Arena-Hard with 9 judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM yields nominally higher rank agreement with human preferences ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$; not significant at $N = 8$) and substantially improved predictive fit ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD} = -55.7$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$). Our contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item The G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees and quantified error propagation bounds for staged estimation (Section~\\ref{sec:method}).\n    \\item Synthetic validation confirming 51.2\\% RMSE reduction over sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$; Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n    \\item On Arena-Hard, the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity within a measurement-theoretic framework (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 7,

(b)~G-MFRM three-stage joint calibration: Stage~1 fits a standard MFRM for initial estimates $(\\hat{\\theta}, \\hat{b}, \\hat{d})$; Stage~2 estimates per-item exposure $e_i$ from external evidence (reference model anchoring, cross-model agreement, and optionally LogProber); Stage~3 performs joint MCMC inference with $e_i$ fixed, recovering contamination-corrected ability $\\tilde{\\theta}_n$, contamination susceptibility $c_n$, and judge severity $d_j$.}",
          "+\\caption{(a)~Sequential pipelines address judge bias and contamination independently; omitted variable bias confounds the unmodeled facet into ability estimates. (b)~G-MFRM three-stage joint calibration: Stage~1 fits a standard MFRM for initial estimates $(\\hat{\\theta}, \\hat{b}, \\hat{d})$; Stage~2 estimates per-item exposure $e_i$ from external evidence (reference model anchoring, cross-model agreement, and optionally LogProber); Stage~3 performs joint MCMC inference with $e_i$ fixed, recovering contamination-corrected ability $\\hat{\\theta}_n$, contamination susceptibility $c_n$, and judge severity $d_j$.}",
          " \\label{fig:framework}",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JUt89jDxVDvmZcbv5Mtz8A`</summary>

```text
1	The evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with XX.X\% fill rate (XX{,}XXX of XX{,}XXX potential cells); sparser designs may compromise identifiability of $c_n$ for models with few observed exposed items, and sparse per-item observations produce elevated item-level mean-square residuals (92/176 items), though model- and judge-level fit remains acceptable. Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), whose validity should be periodically re-assessed as benchmarks age.
2	
3	Empirical CI coverage differs by parameter type: ability estimates $\theta$ approach nominal coverage (${\sim}$90\% at $N = 8$), while contamination susceptibility $c_n$ exhibits posterior overconfidence (71\% at $N = 50$, 68\% at $N = 8$; \citealp{talts2018sbc}), a structural issue from the multiplicative coupling $c_n \cdot e_i$ and the HalfNormal boundary constraint at $c_n = 0$ that does not improve with sample size; $c_n$ credible intervals should be treated as lower bounds on uncertainty (Appendix~\ref{app:ci_coverage}), with recalibration via simulation-based calibration (SBC) as a direction for future work. The bilinear term also assumes rank-1 structure---a single contamination dimension per model---while contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\ reasoning), requiring a higher-rank decomposition $\sum_r c_{nr} \cdot e_{ir}$ that the current model does not capture.
4	
5	Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \approx 17$ for 80\% power to detect $\Delta\tau = 0.071$; the $N = 8$ evaluation has ${\sim}$50\% power, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result. Generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. While self-evaluation sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:self_eval}) confirms robust rankings when excluding self-judging observations, removing overlapping models entirely from the judge panel has not been conducted.
6	
7	
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced G-MFRM, which jointly estimates model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility by formalizing their confounding as omitted variable bias. On Arena-Hard, G-MFRM produces the first joint contamination--severity diagnostic within a measurement-theoretic framework, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$ vs.\ $0.786$; not significant at $N = 8$); well-powered validation requires $N \geq 17$ (Limitations). Treating judge bias and contamination as separable leads to systematic estimation errors; future LLM evaluation frameworks should model these facets jointly.
5	
```

6	\begin{figure*}[t]

8	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_framework_v2}
9	\caption{(a)~Sequential pipelines address judge bias and contamination independently; omitted variable bias confounds the unmodeled facet into ability estimates. (b)~G-MFRM three-stage joint calibration: Stage~1 fits a standard MFRM for initial estimates $(\hat{\theta}, \hat{b}, \hat{d})$; Stage~2 estimates per-item exposure $e_i$ from external evidence (reference model anchoring, cross-model agreement, and optionally LogProber); Stage~3 performs joint MCMC inference with $e_i$ fixed, recovering contamination-corrected ability $\hat{\theta}_n$, contamination susceptibility $c_n$, and judge severity $d_j$.}
10	\label{fig:framework}
11	\end{figure*}

1	The evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with 66.0\% fill rate (18{,}116 of 27{,}456 potential cells); sparser designs may compromise identifiability of $c_n$ for models with few observed exposed items, and sparse per-item observations produce elevated item-level mean-square residuals (92/176 items), though model- and judge-level fit remains acceptable. Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), whose validity should be periodically re-assessed as benchmarks age.
